1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.
8	
9	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\cref{tab:models}).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Arch.} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Layers} & \textbf{Hidden} & \textbf{$n$ (total\,/\,test)} \\
17	\midrule
18	R1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\,/\,43 \\
19	OT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\,/\,47 \\
20	R1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\,/\,20 \\
21	QwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\,/\,19 \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}}
24	\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}
25	\label{tab:models}
26	\end{table}
27

29	\label{sec:probe-architecture}
30	
31	\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\mathbf{h}_t^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Layer selection is analyzed in \cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}

_source line 5371 · 2026-05-18T12:04:49.611319+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.
4	The central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while threshold-dependent metrics excluding crossing rate consistently select deep layers where all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni correction (precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over MiniLM-384d). At 7--8B the advantage is robust; at 32B, model-specific caveats apply (QwQ-32B is predominantly position-mediated). The advantage is encoder-conditional: it narrows substantially against BGE-large-1024d (1/4 models significant).
5	Future work should close the 85.5\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques~\citep{meng2022rome}, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.
6

  "old_string": "With principled layer selection, HS probes achieve precision gains of +15.4 to +32.5 percentage points over 384d sentence embeddings across all four models (Holm-Bonferroni corrected). This advantage is encoder-conditional: against a competitive 1024d encoder, only 1/4 models retain significance under Holm-Bonferroni, and the directional advantage narrows substantially. At 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, sample-limited), model-specific caveats further qualify the results.",
  "new_string": "With principled layer selection, HS probes achieve precision gains of +15.4 to +32.5 percentage points over 384d sentence embeddings across all four models (Holm-Bonferroni corrected), though QwQ-32B's advantage is predominantly position-mediated (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). PCA dimensionality matching to 384d preserves the advantage (all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni), ruling out a pure capacity explanation. Against a competitive 1024d encoder, all four models retain directional HS advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni correction. The advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude that narrows with stronger text baselines. At 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, sample-limited), model-specific caveats further qualify the results."
}

  "old_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-conditional directional advantage.} Against MiniLM-384d, all four models show significant HS precision gains (+15.4 to +32.5pp, Holm-Bonferroni corrected). The advantage persists after PCA dimensionality matching (all four precision tests pass at 384d) but narrows substantially against BGE-large-1024d (1/4 models retain significance), so the advantage is encoder-conditional rather than absolute (\\cref{fig:forest_precision}). At 7--8B scale (moderate samples, robust across 5 seeds), evidence is strong; at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$ test traces, sample-limited), model-specific caveats apply: QwQ-32B's HS advantage is largely position-mediated (residualized probe drops 17.7pp), and the remaining precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) margins over the position baseline are not statistically significant at $n{=}19$ (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).",
  "new_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-conditional directional advantage.} Against MiniLM-384d, all four models show significant HS precision gains (+15.4 to +32.5pp, Holm-Bonferroni corrected). PCA reduction to matched 384d dimensionality (79--92\\% variance retained) preserves significance on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp), ruling out capacity gap as the sole explanation. Against BGE-large-1024d, all four models retain directional advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni (\\cref{fig:forest_precision}). The advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect (survives dimensionality matching) and an encoder-dependent magnitude (narrows with stronger baselines). At 7--8B scale (moderate samples, robust across 5 seeds), evidence is strong; at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$ test traces, sample-limited), model-specific caveats apply: QwQ-32B's HS advantage is largely position-mediated (residualized probe drops 17.7pp), and the remaining precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) margins over the position baseline are not statistically significant at $n{=}19$ (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis})."
}

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Cross-model divergence within identical architecture.}",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}\nPart of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. PCA retains directions of maximum variance in hidden-state space, which need not align with the semantic dimensions captured by trained text encoders, so this control is necessary but not sufficient. A stronger 1024d encoder closes most of the significance gap (1/4 HB-significant), suggesting a continuum rather than a binary modality distinction. The HS advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude component that narrows with stronger baselines.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model divergence within identical architecture.}"
}
```

We do not independently evaluate inter-annotator agreement (IAA). If CP annotation error is ${\\geq}2$ steps, the reported lead times (+4.5 to +13 steps) may partially fall within annotation noise, particularly at the lower end of this range. Future work should quantify annotation reliability and assess its impact on probe evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}"
}
```

_source line 5419 · 2026-05-18T12:11:24.229924+00:00_

17	\paragraph{Design implications for safety monitoring.}
18	If future work achieves acceptable false positive rates, our results suggest temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection (balanced accuracy or precision, never crossing rate) as a starting configuration. Evidence robustness is scale-dependent: at 7--8B, results are robust across 5 random seeds (precision std ${<}8$pp); at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$), higher variance (R1-32B precision std $= 12.8$pp) and model-specific caveats warrant a sample-limited interpretation. At 7--8B, HS probes Pareto-dominate text probes (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). At 32B, practical trade-offs require model-specific decisions (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). All methods produce high safe-trace FPR (39--52\%), possibly because safe traces contain commitment-like state transitions (e.g., the model deciding to refuse) that probes cannot distinguish from harmful commitment. The 85.5\% oracle gap (\cref{sec:oracle}) confirms that passive monitoring captures a small portion of available signal; hidden states encode the model's current safety state but carry limited predictive power about future trajectory evolution. PCA dimensionality control and control task selectivity provide indirect evidence against textual leakage as the sole explanation for the HS advantage; direct erasure methods (e.g., LEACE~\citep{belrose2023leace}) remain untested.
19

_source line 5423 · 2026-05-18T12:11:34.076934+00:00_

10	
11	\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}
12	Part of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. PCA retains directions of maximum variance in hidden-state space, which need not align with the semantic dimensions captured by trained text encoders, so this control is necessary but not sufficient. A stronger 1024d encoder closes most of the significance gap (1/4 HB-significant), suggesting a continuum rather than a binary modality distinction. The HS advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude component that narrows with stronger baselines.
13	
14	\paragraph{Cross-model divergence within identical architecture.}

_source line 5429 · 2026-05-18T12:12:51.009784+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study using crossing rate for layer selection. Validation on other probing tasks is needed to confirm generality.
5	
6	\paragraph{Recommendations for probing methodology.} We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. Even among unbiased metrics, the choice between threshold-based and threshold-free criteria affects layer selection: AUROC tends to select layers at shallower depth (3--50\%) than threshold-dependent metrics (44--98\%). Pure balanced accuracy exhibits the same pattern, selecting layers at 47--73\% depth for 32B models rather than the 94--98\% selected by threshold-dependent metrics. This divergence between threshold-free and threshold-dependent criteria further demonstrates that layer selection methodology is a critical researcher degree of freedom. At AUROC-optimal layers, HS probes retain directional precision advantage on all four mode

_source line 5447 · 2026-05-18T12:13:35.877534+00:00_

92	\subsection{Temporal Context}\label{sec:temporal}
93	
94	Temporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities on all four models (\cref{fig:window_ablation}). Text precision improves +8 to +23pp from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$; HS precision improves +1.7 to +12.9pp. At 7--8B, the modality advantage (+22 to +33pp precision) exceeds the temporal improvement (+13pp). Both effects are complementary: the most robust deployment default is temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection. Full per-model temporal ablations appear in \cref{app:temporal_details}.
95

_source line 5449 · 2026-05-18T12:13:36.129825+00:00_

419	The best BOCPD configuration ($\lambda{=}10$) achieves 93.0\% detection rate with +3.45 steps lead. The simple threshold baseline achieves 65.1\% detection with superior lead (+4.89 steps). All CUSUM configurations underperform the simple threshold on lead time.
420	
421	These results reveal a coverage-timing tradeoff. BOCPD ($\lambda{=}10$) achieves 28pp higher detection rate than simple thresholding (93.0\% vs.\ 65.1\%), but with lower lead (+3.45 vs.\ +4.89 steps). The transition toward unsafe reasoning is gradual rather than abrupt, so change-point methods cannot exploit discontinuities for early warning. When missed detections are costly, BOCPD's coverage advantage justifies its use despite the timing penalty. When early intervention is the priority, simple thresholding provides better lead time.
422

### Tool result

_source line 5454 · 2026-05-18T12:13:59.267713+00:00_

1	Reasoning models with chain-of-thought can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. We present the first systematic comparison of hidden-state (HS) probes and text-based monitors for detecting this safety commitment, evaluated across four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (7--8B, 32B).
2	
3	Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric can reverse a study's conclusions. A composite metric that incorporates crossing rate (the frequency of threshold crossings) systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale and masks the HS advantage entirely. With principled layer selection, HS probes achieve precision gains of +15.4 to +32.5 percentage points over 384d sentence embeddings across all four models (Holm-Bonferroni corrected), though QwQ-32B's advantage is predominantly position-mediated (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). PCA dimensionality matching to 384d preserves the advantage (all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni), ruling out a pure capacity explanation. Against a competitive 1024d encoder, all four models retain directional HS advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni correction. The advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude that narrows with stronger text baselines. At 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, sample-limited), model-specific caveats further qualify the results.
4	
5	Our results characterize the safety commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 39--52\%, and no alternative method consistently matches supervised probing. Layer selection methodology is a critical, underexamined researcher degree of freedom in the probing literature.
6

_source line 5471 · 2026-05-18T12:15:29.320262+00:00_

4	Reasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.
5	
6	\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\cite{selfjailbreak}. Reasoning models can autonomously jailbreak other LLMs with 97\% success~\cite{hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak}, and can be manipulated through self-generated reasoning artifacts~\cite{xu2025manipulated}. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff}, a phenomenon our temporal probes target. Refusal dynamics in standard LLMs~\cite{hu2026refusal_dynamics} and information leakage in traces~\cite{ahrend2026safer_traces} provide additional context.
7	
8	\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} proposes a dedicated lightweight guard model; \citet{su2025reasoning_tokens} unify reasoning tokens and safety in a single framework.
9	
10	\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
13	Internal activations can detect safety violat

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Recommendations for probing methodology.} We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. Even among unbiased metrics, the choice between threshold-based and threshold-free criteria affects layer selection: AUROC tends to select layers at shallower depth (3--50\\%) than threshold-dependent metrics (44--98\\%). At AUROC-optimal layers, HS probes retain directional precision advantage on all four models (+6.2 to +23.0pp), though at lower absolute performance. The cost of ablation is modest (minutes added to an experiment taking hours), and the alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect methodology rather than the phenomenon under study.",

  "old_string": "Information-theoretic reasoning suggests that hidden states (4096 to 5120 dimensions of internal computation) should dominate text features (384-dimensional sentence embeddings) purely by signal capacity. Both temporal context and modality choice significantly improve detection, but their relative magnitudes differ by scale. At 7--8B, the modality advantage exceeds the temporal improvement (\\cref{sec:temporal}), so modality choice matters more than temporal context at this scale; at 32B, the relative magnitudes are model-dependent. Text probes benefit more from temporal context than HS probes because they start from a weaker single-step baseline. Safety commitment is gradual: the model progressively consolidates its intent over multiple reasoning steps rather than switching abruptly.",

_source line 5515 · 2026-05-18T12:19:18.753447+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study using crossing rate for layer selection. Validation on other probing tasks is needed to confirm generality.
5	
6	\paragraph{Recommendations for probing methodology.} We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. The cost of ablation is modest (minutes added to an experiment taking hours), and the alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect methodology rather than the phenomenon under study.
7	
8	\paragraph{Temporal context and modality choice.}
9	Both temporal context and modality choice improve detection, but their relative magnitudes differ by scale. At 7--8B, the modality advantage exceeds the temporal improvement (\cref{sec:temporal}); at 32B, the relative magnitudes are model-dependent. Text probes benefit more from temporal context than HS probes because they start from a weaker single-step baseline. The practic

{

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Cross-model divergence within identical architecture.}\nR1-32B and QwQ-32B share the same base architecture but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}). Training method (R1 distillation vs.\\ native reasoning) is the likely differentiator, since architecture and scale are held constant. Per-model validation is essential: a system validated on one 32B model would fail on the other.\n\n\\paragraph{Design implications for safety monitoring.}\nIf future work achieves acceptable false positive rates, our results suggest temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection (balanced accuracy or precision, never crossing rate) as a starting configuration. Evidence robustness is scale-dependent: at 7--8B, results are robust across 5 random seeds (precision std ${<}8$pp); at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$), higher variance (R1-32B precision std $= 12.8$pp) and model-specific caveats warrant a sample-limited interpretation. At 7--8B, HS probes Pareto-dominate text probes (\\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). At 32B, practical trade-offs require model-specific decisions (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). All methods produce high safe-trace FPR (39--52\\%), possibly because safe traces contain commitment-like state transitions (e.g., the model deciding to refuse) that probes cannot distinguish from harmful commitment. The 85.5\\% oracle gap (\\cref{sec:oracle}) confirms that passive monitoring captures a small portion of available signal; hidden states encode the model's current safety state but carry limited predictive power about future trajectory evolution. PCA capacity control and control task selectivity provide indirect evidence against textual leakage as the sole explanation for the HS advantage; direct erasure methods (e.g., LEACE~\\citep{belrose2023leace}) remain untested.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Cross-model divergence and design implications.}\nR1-32B and QwQ-32B share architecture but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}), with training method (distillation vs.\\ native reasoning) as the likely differentiator; per-model validation is essential. If future work achieves acceptable false positive rates, our results suggest temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) with principled layer selection as a starting configuration. At 7--8B, HS probes Pareto-dominate text probes, robust across 5 seeds (precision std ${<}8$pp); at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$), higher variance (R1-32B std $= 12.8$pp) warrants sample-limited interpretation (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Safe-trace FPR of 39--52\\% and the 85.5\\% oracle gap (\\cref{sec:oracle}) confirm that current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than provide operational detection. PCA capacity control and control task selectivity provide indirect evidence against textual leakage; direct erasure methods (LEACE; \\citealp{belrose2023leace}) remain untested."
}

_source line 5531 · 2026-05-18T12:22:45.182736+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.
4	The central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while threshold-dependent metrics excluding crossing rate consistently select deep layers where all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni correction (precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over MiniLM-384d). At 7--8B the advantage is robust; at 32B, model-specific caveats apply (QwQ-32B is predominantly position-mediated). The advantage is encoder-conditional: it narrows substantially against BGE-large-1024d (1/4 models significant).
5	Future work should close the 85.5\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques~\citep{meng2022rome}, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.
6

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}\nLayer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study using crossing rate for layer selection. Validation on other probing tasks is needed to confirm generality.\n\n\\paragraph{Recommendations for probing methodology.} We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. The cost of ablation is modest (minutes added to an experiment taking hours), and the alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect methodology rather than the phenomenon under study.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}\nLayer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria and that multi-metric ablations become standard practice."
}

_source line 5545 · 2026-05-18T12:25:04.972777+00:00_

1	Reasoning models with chain-of-thought can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. We present the first systematic comparison of hidden-state (HS) probes and text-based monitors for detecting this safety commitment, evaluated across four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (7--8B, 32B).
2	
3	Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric can reverse a study's conclusions. A composite metric that incorporates crossing rate (the frequency of threshold crossings) systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale and masks the HS advantage entirely. With principled layer selection, HS probes achieve precision gains of +15.4 to +32.5 percentage points over 384d sentence embeddings across all four models (Holm-Bonferroni corrected), though QwQ-32B's advantage is predominantly position-mediated (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). PCA dimensionality matching to 384d preserves the advantage (all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni), ruling out a pure capacity explanation. Against a competitive 1024d encoder, all four models retain directional HS advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni correction. The advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude that narrows with stronger text baselines. At 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, sample-limited), model-specific caveats further qualify the results.
4	
5	Our results characterize the safety commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 39--52\%, and no alternative method consistently matches supervised probing. Layer selection methodology is a critical, underexamined researcher degree of freedom in the probing literature.
6

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.
4	The central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while threshold-dependent metrics excluding crossing rate consistently select deep layers where all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni correction (precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over MiniLM-384d). At 7--8B the advantage is robust; at 32B, model-specific caveats apply (QwQ-32B is predominantly position-mediated). The advantage is encoder-conditional: it narrows substantially against BGE-large-1024d (1/4 models significant).
5	Future work should close the 85.5\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques~\citep{meng2022rome}, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.
6

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria and that multi-metric ablations become standard practice.
5	
6	\paragraph{Temporal context and modality choice.}
7	Both temporal context and modality choice improve detection, but their relative magnitudes differ by scale: at 7--8B, the modality advantage exceeds temporal improvement (\cref{sec:temporal}); at 32B, relative magnitudes are model-dependent. Temporal windowing ($W{=}3$ to $15$) provides consistent improvement regardless of modality, while modality choice requires principled layer selection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}
10	Part of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance (\cref{app:control}).

90	R1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early with a +14.2pp margin over the position baseline, which confirms genuine representational signal, but covers only 25\% of traces at the single significant window ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves statistical significance at all window sizes with 74\% coverage, but HS probes fire later than text probes (negative lead time). The position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy than HS probes (0.832 vs.\ 0.796) because harmful steps tend to occur later in traces. HS probes achieve directionally higher precision (+4.5pp: 0.564 vs.\ 0.519, $p{=}0.133$) and lower FPR (+2.5pp: 0.217 vs.\ 0.241, $p{=}0.279$), but neither difference is statistically significant at $n{=}19$. A position-residualized probe (regressing out $t/T$ from hidden states before classification) reduces QwQ-32B balanced accuracy by 17.7pp (from 0.796 to 0.619), but remains significantly above chance (per-trace balanced accuracy 0.667, $p{<}0.003$, Wilcoxon signed-rank; \cref{app:position_residual}). Hidden states encode some position-independent safety signal, but the majority of the HS probe's discriminative power on this model derives from temporal position encoding. The divergence implies that per-model validation is essential: no single detection configuration generalizes even within the same architecture at the same scale, and a system validated on one 32B model would fail on the other.

7	We present the first systematic evaluation across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen) and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations are highly compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers), so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage. Threshold-dependent metrics excluding crossing rate consistently select deep layers (\cref{tab:layer_selection}), where all four models show significant HS advantage (Holm-Bonferroni corrected, \cref{sec:evaluation}; precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over 384d sentence embeddings; \cref{tab:hs_vs_text}).
8

Against BGE-large-1024d, all four models retain directional advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni (\cref{fig:forest_precision}). 1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{32B model-specific caveats.}
4	R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\% coverage, 5/20 traces); dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents larger windows. QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ HS 0.796), HS lead time is negative, and position-residualized probing reduces balanced accuracy by 17.7pp. The QwQ-32B HS advantage is largely position-mediated. Multi-seed analysis (\cref{app:multi_seed}) confirms scale-dependent robustness: 7--8B precision std ${<}8$pp across 5 seeds, while R1-32B std $= 12.8$pp. This motivates a two-tier framing: 7--8B results are robust; 32B results are sample-limited with caveats.
5	
6	\paragraph{High false positive rate and oracle gap.}
7	Safe-trace FPR is 39--52\% at standard thresholds, so current probes characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap confirms that passive probing captures only a fraction of available signal.
8	
9	\paragraph{Cumulative hyperparameter selection.}
10	Layer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class validation traces at 32B scale), yielding up to $64 \times 7 \times 5 = 2{,}240$ configurations. $C$-insensitivity (${<}2$pp) covers one hyperparameter; the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom are substantial, particularly at 32B where per-fold sample sizes are small. $K$ was set as a design pre-selection (\cref{app:k_ablation}), and the $K \times W$ joint surface is not fully explored. Joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified via grid search for QwQ-32B ($W \in \{1,3,5\}$; \cref{app:layer_sweep}), but full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work. Bootstrap CIs at $n{=}19$--$20$ may exhibit below-nominal coverage~\citep{efron1993bootstrap}; $p$-value resolution with 10{,}000 resamples is $10^{-4}$.

…[truncated 1014 chars]

590	
591	
592	\section{QwQ-32B Position-Residualized Probe}
593	\label{app:position_residual}
594	
595	To quantify position-independent safety signal in QwQ-32B hidden states, we regress out the normalized position $t/T$ from each hidden-state vector before training the logistic regression probe. The residualized probe achieves pooled step-level balanced accuracy of 0.619 and per-trace balanced accuracy of 0.667 (16/19 traces with BA $> 0.5$). Three complementary significance tests confirm the residualized probe performs above chance: Wilcoxon signed-rank $p{=}0.0024$, paired $t$-test $p{=}0.0008$, permutation test $p{<}0.001$. The bootstrap 95\% CI for per-trace balanced accuracy is $[0.575, 0.744]$.
596	
597	These results confirm that hidden states encode some position-independent safety signal. However, the drop from full-probe balanced accuracy (0.796) to residualized (0.619, pooled) indicates that the majority of discriminative power derives from temporal position encoding. The HS vs.\ position-baseline precision gap (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR gap (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) are not statistically significant at $n{=}19$, so the non-positional component, while real, is too small for reliable detection at this sample size.
598	
599	
600	\section{Multi-Seed Stability}
601	\label{app:multi_seed}
602	
603	\cref{tab:multi_seed} reports LR probe performance across 5 random seeds at each model's corrected layer and optimal $W$.
604

88	\end{table}
89	
90	R1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early with a +14.2pp margin over the position baseline, which confirms genuine representational signal, but covers only 25\% of traces at the single significant window ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves statistical significance at all window sizes with 74\% coverage, but HS probes fire later than text probes (negative lead time). The position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy than HS probes (0.832 vs.\ 0.796) because harmful steps tend to occur later in traces. HS probes achieve directionally higher precision (+4.5pp: 0.564 vs.\ 0.519, $p{=}0.133$) and lower FPR (+2.5pp: 0.217 vs.\ 0.241, $p{=}0.279$), but neither difference is statistically significant at $n{=}19$. A position-residualized probe (regressing out $t/T$ from hidden states before classification) reduces QwQ-32B balanced accuracy by 17.7pp (from 0.796 to 0.619), but remains significantly above chance (per-trace balanced accuracy 0.667, $p{<}0.003$, Wilcoxon signed-rank; \cref{app:position_residual}). Hidden states encode some position-independent safety signal, but the majority of the HS probe's discriminative power on this model derives from temporal position encoding. The divergence implies that per-model validation is essential: no single detection configuration generalizes even within the same architecture at the same scale, and a system validated on one 32B model would fail on the other.
91	
92	\subsection{Temporal Context}\label{sec:temporal}

[Director 指令] [Patch] Position mediation framing 更新 + 2 个 lingering fixes

## 1. Position Mediation 段落更新（D039）

paper_framing.md 已更新 Position Mediation Analysis 小节。论文需要相应修改：

**核心变化**：D038 的 "7-8B 比 32B 更依赖 position" 结论被 D039 推翻。W=15 残差化数据显示所有模型 position drop 可比（12-18pp），无 scale-dependent 差异。

论文应写：
- "Position is a significant component of probe performance across all models (12-18pp BA reduction upon residualization at optimal W), but all models retain substantial non-positional signal (residualized BA 0.61-0.67, all p < 0.001)"
- "Temporal windowing preserves non-positional signal: larger windows reduce position dependence (R1-8B: 24pp drop at W=3 vs 16pp at W=15)"
- 删除任何 "position-independent" 措辞，也不要说 "predominantly position-mediated"
- HS residualized > text residualized (+3.7 到 +6.9pp)——HS 的非 position 信号确实更强

数据表格见 paper_framing.md "Position Mediation Analysis" 小节。

## 2. Lingering Fixes（第三次提醒，必须本次完成）

**Fix A**: R1-8B FPR "1/5 p<0.05" → "2/5 p<0.05"
- 位置：appendix 约 L624 + experiments.tex 约 L63
- 搜索 "1/5" 附近有 FPR 上下文的位置

**Fix B**: Introduction 约 L13 "retains" → "retain"（主语复数）

这两个 fix 已被 Reviewer 连续 3 轮标记，优先级最高。

## 不需要验证引用。

62	
63	Against the 384d baseline, HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), though R1-8B FPR significance is fragile across seeds (2/5 uncorrected, 1/5 Bonferroni-corrected; \cref{tab:r18b_fpr_seeds}). At 7--8B, the evidence is strong: precision gains of +32.5pp (R1-8B) and +21.9pp (OT-7B), stable across seeds (std ${<}4$pp). R1-8B FPR advantage is directionally consistent across seeds (5/5) but not robust to data split (2/5 reach $p{<}0.05$ uncorrected; after Bonferroni correction for 5 seeds, 1/5 remains significant; \cref{app:multi_seed}). At 32B, both models are significant (HB corrected) but with model-specific caveats: R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage (5/20 traces; significance at this coverage means the probe fires reliably on a narrow subset rather than detecting commitment broadly), and QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance (\cref{app:control}).

623	
624	\paragraph{R1-8B FPR per-seed breakdown.} \cref{tab:r18b_fpr_seeds} reports per-seed HS vs.\ text FPR comparisons for R1-8B. The HS advantage is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds), with 2/5 reaching $p{<}0.05$ uncorrected; after Bonferroni correction for 5 seeds ($\alpha{=}0.01$), 1/5 remains significant. Text FPR is highly stable (std $= 0.007$) while HS FPR is split-sensitive (std $= 0.040$), which explains why the canonical-split FPR advantage ($-7.4$pp) does not generalize robustly across seeds.

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.
8	
9	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
10	
11	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. Three of four metrics excluding crossing rate (precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite) consistently select deep layers: L60 (94\%) and L63 (98\%). Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects middle layers (L30 and L47), consistent with the AUROC pattern discussed in \cref{sec:discussion}. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all four models (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently o

_source line 5629 · 2026-05-18T12:49:58.852897+00:00_

1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{32B model-specific caveats.}
4	R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\% coverage, 5/20 traces); dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents larger windows. QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ HS 0.796), HS lead time is negative, and position-residualized probing reduces balanced accuracy by 17.7pp. Multi-seed analysis (\cref{app:multi_seed}) confirms scale-dependent robustness: 7--8B precision std ${<}8$pp across 5 seeds, while R1-32B std $= 12.8$pp. This motivates a two-tier framing: 7--8B results are robust; 32B results are sample-limited with caveats.
5	
6	\paragraph{High false positive rate and oracle gap.}
7	Safe-trace FPR is 39--52\% at standard thresholds, so current probes characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap confirms that passive probing captures only a fraction of available signal.
8	
9	\paragraph{Cumulative hyperparameter selection.}
10	Layer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class validation traces at 32B scale), yielding up to $64 \times 7 \times 5 = 2{,}240$ configurations. $C$-insensitivity (${<}2$pp) covers one hyperparameter; the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom are substantial, particularly at 32B where per-fold sample sizes are small. $K$ was set as a design pre-selection (\cref{app:k_ablation}), and the $K \times W$ joint surface is not fully explored. Joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified via grid search for QwQ-32B ($W \in \{1,3,5\}$; \cref{app:layer_sweep}), but full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work. Bootstrap CIs at $n{=}19$--$20$ may exhibit below-nominal coverage~\citep{efron1993bootstrap}; $p$-value resolution with 10{,}000 resamples is $10^{-4}$.
11	
12	\paragraph{CP annotation uncertainty.}

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria and that multi-metric ablations become standard practice.
5	
6	\paragraph{Temporal context and modality choice.}
7	Both temporal context and modality choice improve detection, but their relative magnitudes differ by scale: at 7--8B, the modality advantage exceeds temporal improvement (\cref{sec:temporal}); at 32B, relative magnitudes are model-dependent. Temporal windowing ($W{=}3$ to $15$) provides consistent improvement regardless of modality, while modality choice requires principled layer selection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}
10	Part of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. 590	
591	
592	\section{QwQ-32B Position-Residualized Probe}
593	\label{app:position_residual}
594	
595	To quantify position-independent safety signal in QwQ-32B hidden states, we regress out the normalized position $t/T$ from each hidden-state vector before training the logistic regression probe. The residualized probe achieves pooled step-level balanced accuracy of 0.619 and per-trace balanced accuracy of 0.667 (16/19 traces with BA $> 0.5$). Three complementary significance tests confirm the residualized probe performs above chance: Wilcoxon signed-rank $p{=}0.0024$, paired $t$-test $p{=}0.0008$, permutation test $p{<}0.001$. The bootstrap 95\% CI for per-trace balanced accuracy is $[0.575, 0.744]$.
596	
597	These results confirm that hidden states encode some position-independent safety signal. However, the drop from full-probe balanced accuracy (0.796) to residualized (0.619, pooled) indicates that the majority of discriminative power derives from temporal position encoding. The HS vs.\ position-baseline precision gap (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR gap (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) are not statistically significant at $n{=}19$, so the non-positional component, while real, is too small for reliable detection at this sample size.
598	
599	
600	\section{Multi-Seed Stability}
601	\label{app:multi_seed}
602	
603	\cref{tab:multi_seed} reports LR probe performance across 5 random seeds at each model's corrected layer and optimal $W$.
604

_source line 5663 · 2026-05-18T13:54:37.685526+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch — R12 综合重写] D042 Final Framing + 写作策略重构

投稿目标: EMNLP 2026 ARR May cycle, DDL May 25。

## 核心 Framing 变更 (D042)

R12 审稿 Overall 3.0 (Findings)。3 个 Must-Fix 要求 framing 重构。Phase 1 补充实验已全部完成，数据结果改变了原计划的分层方式。

### 1. 不再使用 "all four models" 或 "7-8B robust / 32B sample-limited"

5-seed HB 数据显示 robustness 按模型分化，非按 scale：
- OT-7B: 5/5 HB, ΔPrec +20.9±1.3pp
- R1-32B: 5/5 HB, ΔPrec +13.7±2.0pp
- QwQ-32B: 5/5 HB, ΔPrec +19.9±2.5pp
- R1-8B: 3/5 HB (FPR unstable), ΔPrec +14.6±4.5pp

R1-32B 统计上比 R1-8B 更稳定。"7-8B robust / 32B sample-limited" 错误。

### 2. Position residualization: 3/4 模型 HS 优势持续，R1-32B 例外

| Model | Resid HS-Text Gap | p | HB |
|-------|-------------------|-------|-----|
| OT-7B | +6.9pp | <0.001 | ✅ |
| R1-8B | +6.4pp (W=15) | <0.001 | ✅ |
| QwQ-32B | +3.7pp | 0.011 | ✅ |
| R1-32B | +1.9pp | 0.914 | ❌ |

R1-32B 的 HS 优势几乎完全由 position 信号驱动。

### 3. Encoder-conditional 从 caveat 提升为核心发现 (MF1)

384d 和 1024d 结果并列。Abstract 当前结构已基本合理（Reviewer 确认），微调即可——不要大改 Abstract。重点改 Introduction 和 Conclusion。

## 写作策略指导（基于同类论文调研）

### 叙事线重组

**Introduction hook 调整**：从"self-jailbreak 现象"改为"方法论危机"开场——"Hidden-state probing 被广泛用于安全检测，但层选择这一从未被审查的方法论选择可以逆转结论"。Self-jailbreak 作为高风险测试场景引入（第 2-3 段）。

**"Researcher degrees of freedom" 前置**：当前只在 Discussion 出现，应在 Abstract 和 Introduction 早期引入。Layer selection 是一个 underexamined researcher degree of freedom。

**负面结果 = 表征性质发现**：Section 4.5 开头增加 framing 句——每个方法的失败揭示安全承诺表征的一个具体性质（BOCPD 失败→渐进过程非突变；Mahalanobis→信号在次要 PCA 成分中；RepE→安全表征模型特异）。

**跨模型分歧作为发现**：不是需要辩解的弱点，而是核心发现。"蒸馏训练和原生推理训练产生质性不同的安全表征几何结构"。

## 逐条修改清单

### Must-Fix
- **MF1**: Introduction 和 Conclusion 中 384d+1024d 并列呈现。Abstract 微调措辞（不大改）。
- **MF2a**: 按模型分层（非按 scale）。Per-model evidence tier 写入 Discussion。详见 paper_framing.md "Per-model evidence tier (D042)"。
- **MF2b**: Appendix 添加 5-seed HB 通过率表格（20 行 per-seed 数据 + 4 行汇总）。正文 Section 4.3 或 Discussion 提及 "18/20 (90%) pass full HB across 5 seeds; R1-8B FPR unstable in 2/5 seeds"。
- **MF2c**: R1-32B 标注方式：正文提及 "statistically stable (5/5 HB, ΔPrec std=2.0pp) but advantage is position-mediated (residualized gap NS)"。注意区分 absolute precision std (12.8pp) 和 paired delta std (2.0pp)。
- **MF2d**: Appendix tab:position_residual 更新 QwQ "---" → "0.582"。新增 R1-32B 行（resid HS=0.552, resid text=0.532, gap=+1.9pp, p=0.914）。Discussion 提及 "3/4 models retain non-positional HS advantage; R1-32B is the exception"。

### Should-Fix
- **SF1**: Limitations 强化 CP annotation 量化影响："if annotation error ≥2 steps, QwQ-32B lead time (-1.2 to +1.9) falls entirely within noise"
- **SF2**: Discussion 中 "W=3-15 as starting configuration" 改为条件性语言 "if future work achieves acceptable FPR thresholds, W=3-15 provides a reasonable starting range"
- **SF3**: Conclusion 压缩至 4-5 句，消除与 Discussion 的 60-70% 重叠
- **SF4**: Limitations 增加 R1-32B joint (layer,W) grid search 缺失讨论（根本瓶颈是 n=20）
- **SF5**: 正文或 Appendix 报告 5-seed mean ΔPrec（已包含在 MF2b 表格中）
- **SF6**: Section 4.5 开头增加 framing："These failures are themselves informative: they reveal that safety commitment representations are gradual (ruling out change-point methods), concentrated in minor principal components (ruling out distance-based methods), and model-specific (ruling out universal direction approaches)."

### Minor
- **m1**: Introduction "Naïve reasoning suggests HS should dominate" → 删除或改为中性表述（当前措辞从信息论暗示 HS>text 是预期，削弱 encoder-conditional 发现的 surprise value）
- **m2**: 正文（Section 4.3 或 4.4）增加一句 R1-32B precision std=0.128 + 但 paired ΔPrec std=0.020
- **m3**: Abstract P2 保留 PCA-384d 核心事实（"all four pass HB at matched dimensionality"），删除 "two separable components" 细节
- **m5**: Method 中 K=5 增加 post-hoc caveat
- **m8**: Geiger et al. 2025 bib + Related Work 引用（已由 Agent 完成，验证是否在正文中）

### Discussion 新增
- LLM encoder ceiling 预测句："The encoder-conditional pattern predicts that LLM-based text encoders (e.g., E5-Mistral-7B, 4096d) would further narrow or potentially close the gap, bounding the practical lifetime of any HS advantage claim as text encoding improves."

## 已完成的修改（不要重复做）
- bib "and others" 3 条已修复 ✅
- Geiger et al. 2025 已加入 bib + related_work.tex ✅
- lingering fixes (FPR 1/5→2/5, retains→retain) 已完成 ✅

## 不需要验证引用（本轮已由 Agent 验证）。

_source line 5667 · 2026-05-18T13:54:58.296032+00:00_

1	Reasoning models with chain-of-thought can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. We present the first systematic comparison of hidden-state (HS) probes and text-based monitors for detecting this safety commitment, evaluated across four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (7--8B, 32B).
2	
3	Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric can reverse a study's conclusions. A composite metric that incorporates crossing rate (the frequency of threshold crossings) systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale and masks the HS advantage entirely. With principled layer selection, HS probes achieve precision gains of +15.4 to +32.5 percentage points over 384d sentence embeddings across all four models (Holm-Bonferroni corrected). PCA dimensionality matching to 384d preserves the advantage (all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni), ruling out a pure capacity explanation. Against a competitive 1024d encoder, all four models retain directional HS advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni correction. The advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude that narrows with stronger text baselines. At 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, sample-limited), model-specific caveats further qualify the results.
4	
5	Our results characterize the safety commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 39--52\%, and no alternative method consistently matches supervised probing. Layer selection methodology is a critical, underexamined researcher degree of freedom in the probing literature.
6

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before building production detectors, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides a systematic characterization of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect.
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. A fundamental question remains: should a monitoring system read the model's hidden states or its generated text? This comparison has never been conducted under controlled conditions. Na\"ive reasoning suggests HS features (up to 5120 dimensions) should dominate text embeddings (384d sentence encoder), but no prior work tests this systematically. A further complication is that HS probes require selecting which transformer layer to monitor, a choice rarely scrutinized but potentially decisive.
6

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.
4	The central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while threshold-dependent metrics excluding crossing rate consistently select deep layers where all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni correction (precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over MiniLM-384d). At 7--8B the advantage is robust; at 32B, model-specific caveats apply (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). The advantage is encoder-conditional: it narrows substantially against BGE-large-1024d (1/4 models significant).
5	Future work should close the 85.5\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques~\citep{meng2022rome}, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.
6

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.
8	
9	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
10	
11	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. Three of four metrics excluding crossing rate (precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite) consistently select deep layers: L60 (94\%) and L63 (98\%). Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects middle layers (L30 and L47), consistent with the AUROC pattern discussed in \cref{sec:discussion}. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all four models (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently o
…[truncated 14260 chars]

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding: at biased layers, the HS advantage appears scale-dependent; at corrected layers, all four models are significant (\cref{sec:evaluation}). The mechanism is representation compression at shallow layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), which pushes a linear probe to the noise floor and inflates crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Any composite metric that incorporates crossing rate is susceptible to this bias, and we hypothesize the effect generalizes beyond safety probing to any threshold-based detection study. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria and that multi-metric ablations become standard practice.
5	
6	\paragraph{Temporal context and modality choice.}
7	Both temporal context and modality choice improve detection, but their relative magnitudes differ by scale: at 7--8B, the modality advantage exceeds temporal improvement (\cref{sec:temporal}); at 32B, relative magnitudes are model-dependent. Temporal windowing ($W{=}3$ to $15$) provides consistent improvement regardless of modality, while modality choice requires principled layer selection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}
10	Part of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. A stronger 1024d encoder closes most of th
…[truncated 1527 chars]

1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{32B model-specific caveats.}
4	R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\% coverage, 5/20 traces); dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents larger windows. QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ HS 0.796) and HS lead time is negative. Position residualization reduces balanced accuracy by 12--18pp across all models, though all retain substantial non-positional signal (\cref{app:position_residual}). Multi-seed analysis (\cref{app:multi_seed}) confirms scale-dependent robustness: 7--8B precision std ${<}8$pp across 5 seeds, while R1-32B std $= 12.8$pp. This motivates a two-tier framing: 7--8B results are robust; 32B results are sample-limited with caveats.
5	
6	\paragraph{High false positive rate and oracle gap.}
7	Safe-trace FPR is 39--52\% at standard thresholds, so current probes characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap confirms that passive probing captures only a fraction of available signal.
8	
9	\paragraph{Cumulative hyperparameter selection.}
10	Layer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class validation traces at 32B scale), yielding up to $64 \times 7 \times 5 = 2{,}240$ configurations. $C$-insensitivity (${<}2$pp) covers one hyperparameter; the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom are substantial, particularly at 32B where per-fold sample sizes are small. $K$ was set as a design pre-selection (\cref{app:k_ablation}), and the $K \times W$ joint surface is not fully explored. Joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified via grid search for QwQ-32B ($W \in \{1,3,5\}$; \cref{app:layer_sweep}), but full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work. Bootstrap CIs at $n{=}19$--$20$ may exhibit below-nominal coverage~\citep{efron1993bootstrap}; $p$-value resolu
…[truncated 1057 chars]

1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.
8	
9	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\cref{tab:models}).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Arch.} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Layers} & \textbf{Hidden} & \textbf{$n$ (total\,/\,test)} \\
17	\midrule
18	R1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\,/\,43 \\
19	OT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\,/\,47 \\
20	R1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\,/\,20 \\
21	QwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\,/\,19 \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}}
24	\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}
25	\label{tab:models}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	\subsection{Probe Architecture}
29	\label{sec:probe-architecture}
30	
31	\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\mathbf{h}_t^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Layer selection is analyzed in \cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}

    "old_string": "Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. A fundamental question remains: should a monitoring system read the model's hidden states or its generated text? This comparison has never been conducted under controlled conditions. Na\\\"ive reasoning suggests HS features (up to 5120 dimensions) should dominate text embeddings (384d sentence encoder), but no prior work tests this systematically. A further complication is that HS probes require selecting which transformer layer to monitor, a choice rarely scrutinized but potentially decisive.",
    "new_string": "Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. A fundamental question remains: should a monitoring system read the model's hidden states or its generated text? This comparison has never been conducted under controlled conditions, and a further complication is that HS probes require selecting which transformer layer to monitor. Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that has received almost no scrutiny in the probing literature, yet we show it can reverse a study's central conclusion."
  }
}

  "old_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-conditional directional advantage.} Against MiniLM-384d, all four models show significant HS precision gains (+15.4 to +32.5pp, Holm-Bonferroni corrected). PCA reduction to matched 384d dimensionality (79--92\\% variance retained) preserves significance on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp), ruling out capacity gap as the sole explanation. Against BGE-large-1024d, all four models retain directional advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni (\\cref{fig:forest_precision}). The advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect (survives dimensionality matching) and an encoder-dependent magnitude (narrows with stronger baselines). At 7--8B scale (moderate samples, robust across 5 seeds), evidence is strong; at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$ test traces, sample-limited), model-specific caveats apply (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).",
  "new_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-conditional directional advantage.} Against MiniLM-384d, all four models show significant HS precision gains (+15.4 to +32.5pp, Holm-Bonferroni corrected); PCA reduction to matched 384d (79--92\\% variance retained) preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), ruling out capacity gap. Against BGE-large-1024d, all four models retain directional advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni (\\cref{fig:forest_precision}). Robustness varies by model, not scale: OT-7B (5/5 seeds HB, $\\Delta$Prec $+20.9{\\pm}1.3$pp) and QwQ-32B (5/5 HB, $+19.9{\\pm}2.5$pp) are most stable; R1-8B FPR is seed-fragile (3/5 HB); R1-32B passes all seeds (5/5 HB, $\\Delta$Prec std${=}2.0$pp) but its advantage is position-mediated (residualized HS-text gap NS; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis})."
}

Against a competitive 1024d encoder, all four models retain directional advantage but only 1/4 survives Holm-Bonferroni correction. {
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.\nThe central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while threshold-dependent metrics excluding crossing rate consistently select deep layers where all four models pass Holm-Bonferroni correction (precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over MiniLM-384d). At 7--8B the advantage is robust; at 32B, model-specific caveats apply (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). The advantage is encoder-conditional: it narrows substantially against BGE-large-1024d (1/4 models significant).\nFuture work should close the 85.5\\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques~\\citep{meng2022rome}, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.",
  "new_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models. Layer selection is a critical researcher degree of freedom: a biased composite metric reverses the HS advantage at 32B scale, while principled selection recovers significance on all four models (precision +15.4 to +32.5pp over 384d text, Holm-Bonferroni corrected; 1/4 against 1024d). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage after residualization; R1-32B is the exception. Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon (FPR 39--52\\%, oracle gap 85.5\\%) rather than constitute production detectors. Future work should close the oracle gap through causal interventions~\\citep{meng2022rome} and test whether LLM-based text encoders close the remaining modality gap."
}
```

{

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}\nPart of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. A stronger 1024d encoder closes most of the significance gap (1/4 HB-significant), suggesting a continuum rather than a binary modality distinction. The HS advantage has two separable components: a robust modality effect that survives dimensionality matching, and an encoder-dependent magnitude component that narrows with stronger baselines.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Encoder-conditional advantage.}\nPart of the observed advantage may reflect text encoder quality rather than hidden-state informational superiority. At matched 384d dimensionality via PCA (79--92\\% variance retained), the precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all four model-level precision tests pass Holm-Bonferroni), eliminating capacity gap as the sole explanation. A stronger 1024d encoder closes most of the significance gap (1/4 HB-significant), suggesting a continuum rather than a binary modality distinction. This pattern predicts that LLM-based text encoders (e.g., E5-Mistral-7B, 4096d) would further narrow or close the gap, bounding the practical lifetime of any HS advantage claim as text encoding improves."
}
```

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Cross-model divergence and design implications.}\nR1-32B and QwQ-32B share architecture but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}), with training method (distillation vs.\\ native reasoning) as the likely differentiator; per-model validation is essential. If future work achieves acceptable false positive rates, our results suggest temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) with principled layer selection as a starting configuration. At 7--8B, HS probes Pareto-dominate text probes, robust across 5 seeds (precision std ${<}8$pp); at 32B ($n{=}19$--$20$), higher variance (R1-32B std $= 12.8$pp) warrants sample-limited interpretation (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Safe-trace FPR of 39--52\\% and the 85.5\\% oracle gap (\\cref{sec:oracle}) confirm that current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than provide operational detection. PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, and position residualization (HS retains +3.7 to +6.9pp more non-positional signal than text; \\cref{app:position_residual}) provide converging evidence that the HS advantage reflects genuine safety representations; direct erasure methods (LEACE; \\citealp{belrose2023leace}) remain untested.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Per-model evidence and design implications.}\nRobustness splits by model, not scale. OT-7B provides the cleanest evidence (5/5 seeds HB, largest residualized gap +6.9pp, highest selectivity 18.3pp). QwQ-32B is robust (5/5 HB, residualized gap +3.7pp significant) but its position baseline is competitive. R1-8B precision is stable ($\\Delta$Prec std${=}4.5$pp, residualized gap +6.4pp) but FPR is seed-fragile (3/5 HB). R1-32B is statistically stable (5/5 HB, $\\Delta$Prec std${=}2.0$pp) but its HS advantage is position-mediated: the residualized HS-text gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$), non-significant (\\cref{app:position_residual}). Distillation and native reasoning training thus produce qualitatively different safety representation geometry within the same architecture. If future work achieves acceptable false positive rates, temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) with principled layer selection provides a reasonable starting range. PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, and position residualization (3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage) provide converging evidence that the HS advantage reflects genuine safety representations in most models; direct erasure methods (LEACE; \\citealp{belrose2023leace}) remain untested."
}
```

</details>

  "old_string": "No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing across all four models (\\cref{tab:negative}). We prioritize precision over balanced accuracy as the primary evaluation criterion because in a hypothetical deployment scenario, false positives on pre-CP steps would waste intervention resources (triggering unnecessary safety responses), while missed post-CP steps merely reduce coverage without causing immediate harm. This framing is critical for interpreting RepE results below.",
  "new_string": "No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing across all four models (\\cref{tab:negative}). These failures are themselves informative: they reveal that safety commitment representations are gradual (ruling out change-point methods), concentrated in minor principal components (ruling out distance-based methods), and model-specific (ruling out universal direction approaches). We prioritize precision because in a hypothetical deployment scenario, false positives on pre-CP steps would waste intervention resources, while missed post-CP steps merely reduce coverage."
}

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{32B model-specific caveats.}\nR1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\\% coverage, 5/20 traces); dimensionality ($51200 \\times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents larger windows.
